That’s the part that gets confusing for me, because my doctor has told me I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it in practical ways, like my work pants don’t fit the same anymore. They hang loose on me, and I know that probably means something.\n\nBut at the same time, when I get on the scale and the number is lower, there’s this part of me that feels relieved. Not exactly happy, or maybe I don’t want to call it happy, but relieved, like I did something right or at least didn’t do something wrong. So when people say I’m too thin, I can hear them, and part of me knows they might be right… but another part of me doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It’s like I can see the looseness in my clothes, but I still don’t trust that I’m “too” thin.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that way to myself, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I spend the rest of the day worried it’s going to show on me somehow. Like I’ll feel it sitting there, and then I start checking myself, my stomach or how my clothes feel, and I keep thinking I need to make up for it. Not always in some clear plan, but in my head it’s just going over and over — like, “Why did I eat that?” or “What if this changes my weight?” It makes me scared, and then restricting feels like the safer thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. I don’t always keep track of it in a very organized way, like writing it down every week or anything, but my doctor has said I’ve gotten too thin, and I can tell something has changed.\n\nMy work pants, for example — they used to fit a certain way, and now they hang loose on me. I notice it when I’m moving around at work, because I’m always bending and walking and cleaning, and I’ll have to adjust them more than I used to. So it’s not like I can say I haven’t noticed.\n\nBut then when I see the number on the scale is lower, part of me feels relieved, which is hard to admit. Even if someone else is saying it’s too much, or that I’m too thin, in my own head it doesn’t land the same way. I know that probably sounds strange, but yes, I have lost weight, and other people seem to think it’s significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even if other people tell you it's too low?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I suppose I don’t fully know how to answer that, because if I’m just going by what my doctor says, then no, I guess they don’t think my weight is normal. They’ve told me I’ve gotten too thin. And it’s not like there’s no evidence of it, because my work pants really do hang loose now, and I notice that during the day when I’m cleaning or moving around. I’ll have to pull them up or adjust them, and that didn’t used to happen as much.\n\nBut in my own head, it doesn’t feel as simple as, “Oh, I’m too low.” When I see the number on the scale lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what people are telling me, but it’s more like… emotionally, it doesn’t match. Other people say it’s too low, and I can hear that, but then the scale goes down and there’s this little feeling like, okay, that’s better, that’s safer somehow.\n\nSo I guess I can understand why other people say it’s too low. I just have a hard time feeling that it is.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, exactly. The fear is a big part of it, but sometimes it’s also just become this pattern where I tell myself I don’t need as much as everyone else.\n\nLike, I’ll cook a proper plate for my husband and for my mother-in-law, and I’ll make sure theirs looks normal — enough food, something filling, all of that. But then by the time I sit down, I’ll look at what’s left or think about making my own plate, and I’ll just sort of decide coffee is enough for me, or maybe a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been tasting things while cooking, even if that’s not really a meal.\n\nSo yes, gaining weight is there in the background, but it’s not always this loud thought every single time. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve gotten used to giving other people the real meal and then making excuses for why I only need a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. When you say “much larger,” I picture someone eating a whole lot very quickly, like more than a usual meal, and feeling like it was obviously too much. I can’t really think of times like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel uncomfortable or guilty even after eating what someone else would probably say is a normal amount, but that’s not the same as eating a huge amount in a short time. My sense of what’s “a lot” may not be very reliable, honestly, because even small things can feel like a lot to me. But no, I don’t have clear episodes where I’m eating far more than most people would in one sitting or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you feel hungry for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that probably is the main reason, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that plainly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can’t just eat it and move on with my day. I’ll spend the rest of the day thinking about it, like it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll check myself, or look at how my clothes are sitting, or feel around my waist, and I’ll keep going over it in my head. Like, why did I eat that, was that too much, do I need to make up for it later.\n\nSo even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, well, if I eat more now then I’m going to be anxious all afternoon or all evening. And sometimes it feels easier to stay hungry than to deal with that fear afterward. I know that may not sound very sensible, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I like to admit. I don’t go around saying to myself, “my worth is my weight,” exactly, because that sounds awful and too simple. But the feeling is there.\n\nWhen I look small, or neat, or kind of contained, I feel like I’m doing something right. Like I’ve kept myself under control, like I’m not taking up too much space, like I’ve managed things properly. It gives me this sense that I’m still acceptable, I suppose.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full — even just after eating, even if logically I know bodies do that — it can really change how I feel about myself. I’ll think, oh no, I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve been careless or weak or something. It’s not just “I don’t like how that looks,” it feels more personal than that, like it says something bad about me.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t think I’d ever judge another person that way. But with myself, yes… staying small makes me feel like I’m handling everything right, and any change in my shape can make me feel like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you try to limit how much you eat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, though it’s a big one. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve already decided what I’m “allowed” before I even sit down.\n\nI cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law — I’ll make sure they have enough, like a real meal, and I fuss over whether they need more of this or that. But then when it’s my turn, by the time I actually sit down, I’ll tell myself, oh, I’m fine with coffee, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or maybe half a piece of toast. I make it sound reasonable in my head, like I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been nibbling while cooking, even when that’s not really true.\n\nIt’s strange because I can see what a normal plate looks like when I’m giving it to someone else. I know how to do that. But for me, it suddenly feels like too much, or unnecessary, or like I should be able to get by on less. So yes, the weight fear is there, but there’s also this habit of cutting it down and convincing myself that a very small amount is enough for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you feel hungry for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that probably is the main reason, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that plainly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can’t just eat it and move on with my day. I’ll spend the rest of the day thinking about it, like it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll check myself, or look at how my clothes are sitting, or feel around my waist, and I’ll keep going over it in my head. Like, why did I eat that, was that too much, do I need to make up for it later.\n\nSo even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, well, if I eat more now then I’m going to be anxious all afternoon or all evening. And sometimes it feels easier to stay hungry than to deal with that fear afterward. I know that may not sound very sensible, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I like to admit. I don’t go around saying to myself, “my worth is my weight,” exactly, because that sounds awful and too simple. But the feeling is there.\n\nWhen I look small, or neat, or kind of contained, I feel like I’m doing something right. Like I’ve kept myself under control, like I’m not taking up too much space, like I’ve managed things properly. It gives me this sense that I’m still acceptable, I suppose.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full — even just after eating, even if logically I know bodies do that — it can really change how I feel about myself. I’ll think, oh no, I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve been careless or weak or something. It’s not just “I don’t like how that looks,” it feels more personal than that, like it says something bad about me.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t think I’d ever judge another person that way. But with myself, yes… staying small makes me feel like I’m handling everything right, and any change in my shape can make me feel like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you try to limit how much you eat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, though it’s a big one. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve already decided what I’m “allowed” before I even sit down.\n\nI cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law — I’ll make sure they have enough, like a real meal, and I fuss over whether they need more of this or that. But then when it’s my turn, by the time I actually sit down, I’ll tell myself, oh, I’m fine with coffee, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or maybe half a piece of toast. I make it sound reasonable in my head, like I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been nibbling while cooking, even when that’s not really true.\n\nIt’s strange because I can see what a normal plate looks like when I’m giving it to someone else. I know how to do that. But for me, it suddenly feels like too much, or unnecessary, or like I should be able to get by on less. So yes, the weight fear is there, but there’s also this habit of cutting it down and convincing myself that a very small amount is enough for me.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think you would still try to control your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / janitor_or_building_cleaner / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Restrictive_Intake\nmanifestation: \"I cook plates for my husband and my mother-in-law, but by the time I sit down I usually tell myself coffee, a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast is enough for me.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think… yes, probably. Maybe not in exactly the same way, but I do think I’d still try to keep it controlled.

It’s hard to separate it out, because the weight and shape part is mixed into everything, but there’s also this feeling that I don’t need what other people need, or that I shouldn’t have a full plate like everyone else. Like at home, I’ll cook for my husband and my mother-in-law, and I can make their plates properly without thinking it’s too much. I’ll put food out for them and make sure they’ve had enough.

But then when it comes to me, by the time I sit down, I’ll usually tell myself, oh, coffee is fine, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast is enough. And I can make that sound very sensible to myself in the moment, like I’m not really hungry or I don’t need much. So even if I didn’t care about my shape, I think that habit of cutting it down and keeping it small would still be there.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my regular doctor wanted me to come in because she says I’ve gotten too thin. I guess that’s the main reason. I didn’t really think of it as something for a clinic like this at first, because I still go to work and do what I need to do, but she seemed concerned.\n\nI have noticed things, I suppose. Like my work pants — the ones I wear for cleaning — they’ve been hanging loose on me more than they used to. I keep having to pull them up, and I tell myself they’re just old or stretched out, but… I can see it. And when I get on the scale, if the number is lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds strange, especially when other people are saying I’m too thin, but that’s honestly what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but yes. I do get scared of gaining weight. It’s not even always after some huge meal or anything. If I eat something that feels heavier to me, like biscuits or pasta, then afterward I can’t seem to just move on with the day like a normal person.\n\nI’ll be at work or at home and I keep thinking, “Is it going to show? Can people tell? Is my stomach sticking out?” And I’ll check myself, like how my waistband feels, or I’ll look at myself from the side, or just keep touching at my clothes to see if they feel tighter. It can take over the rest of the day, honestly. Then I start thinking I need to make up for it somehow, because otherwise it feels like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that feels excessive to you in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “excessive” is hard for me to judge, because sometimes even what other people would probably call a normal amount can feel like too much to me.\n\nIf you mean like sitting down and eating a huge amount all at once, I don’t really know that I do that. I can’t think of a clear example where I’d say, yes, that was an unusually large amount in a short time. It’s more that certain foods or portions feel excessive in my head afterward, even if maybe they wouldn’t look that way to someone else. So… I guess I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no, not like that. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating, and I can get really upset in my head about it, but I don’t usually go and make myself vomit.\n\nSometimes I’ve wondered if I’d feel less panicky if I could undo it somehow, but actually doing that isn’t something I can say I do. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably a yes-or-no question, but it’s hard because the feelings after eating can be so strong, even if I’m not vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, do you mean like going for a run or doing a workout specifically because I ate? Because I don’t really have, like, a clear routine where I eat and then say, “Now I have to exercise to burn this off.”\n\nMy work is physical already — I’m on my feet a lot, cleaning and moving around — so sometimes it’s hard for me to separate what’s just part of the day from what would count as exercise. But as far as deliberately exercising right after eating to cancel it out… I don’t know that I’d say I do that, no. It’s more something I worry about in my head than something I can point to as a specific exercise habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are too thin, even when other people tell you that you are?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. That’s the part that gets confusing for me, because my doctor has told me I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it in practical ways, like my work pants don’t fit the same anymore. They hang loose on me, and I know that probably means something.\n\nBut at the same time, when I get on the scale and the number is lower, there’s this part of me that feels relieved. Not exactly happy, or maybe I don’t want to call it happy, but relieved, like I did something right or at least didn’t do something wrong. So when people say I’m too thin, I can hear them, and part of me knows they might be right… but another part of me doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It’s like I can see the looseness in my clothes, but I still don’t trust that I’m “too” thin.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that way to myself, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I spend the rest of the day worried it’s going to show on me somehow. Like I’ll feel it sitting there, and then I start checking myself, my stomach or how my clothes feel, and I keep thinking I need to make up for it. Not always in some clear plan, but in my head it’s just going over and over — like, “Why did I eat that?” or “What if this changes my weight?” It makes me scared, and then restricting feels like the safer thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. I don’t always keep track of it in a very organized way, like writing it down every week or anything, but my doctor has said I’ve gotten too thin, and I can tell something has changed.\n\nMy work pants, for example — they used to fit a certain way, and now they hang loose on me. I notice it when I’m moving around at work, because I’m always bending and walking and cleaning, and I’ll have to adjust them more than I used to. So it’s not like I can say I haven’t noticed.\n\nBut then when I see the number on the scale is lower, part of me feels relieved, which is hard to admit. Even if someone else is saying it’s too much, or that I’m too thin, in my own head it doesn’t land the same way. I know that probably sounds strange, but yes, I have lost weight, and other people seem to think it’s significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even if other people tell you it's too low?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I suppose I don’t fully know how to answer that, because if I’m just going by what my doctor says, then no, I guess they don’t think my weight is normal. They’ve told me I’ve gotten too thin. And it’s not like there’s no evidence of it, because my work pants really do hang loose now, and I notice that during the day when I’m cleaning or moving around. I’ll have to pull them up or adjust them, and that didn’t used to happen as much.\n\nBut in my own head, it doesn’t feel as simple as, “Oh, I’m too low.” When I see the number on the scale lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what people are telling me, but it’s more like… emotionally, it doesn’t match. Other people say it’s too low, and I can hear that, but then the scale goes down and there’s this little feeling like, okay, that’s better, that’s safer somehow.\n\nSo I guess I can understand why other people say it’s too low. I just have a hard time feeling that it is.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, exactly. The fear is a big part of it, but sometimes it’s also just become this pattern where I tell myself I don’t need as much as everyone else.\n\nLike, I’ll cook a proper plate for my husband and for my mother-in-law, and I’ll make sure theirs looks normal — enough food, something filling, all of that. But then by the time I sit down, I’ll look at what’s left or think about making my own plate, and I’ll just sort of decide coffee is enough for me, or maybe a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been tasting things while cooking, even if that’s not really a meal.\n\nSo yes, gaining weight is there in the background, but it’s not always this loud thought every single time. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve gotten used to giving other people the real meal and then making excuses for why I only need a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. When you say “much larger,” I picture someone eating a whole lot very quickly, like more than a usual meal, and feeling like it was obviously too much. I can’t really think of times like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel uncomfortable or guilty even after eating what someone else would probably say is a normal amount, but that’s not the same as eating a huge amount in a short time. My sense of what’s “a lot” may not be very reliable, honestly, because even small things can feel like a lot to me. But no, I don’t have clear episodes where I’m eating far more than most people would in one sitting or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you feel hungry for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that probably is the main reason, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that plainly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can’t just eat it and move on with my day. I’ll spend the rest of the day thinking about it, like it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll check myself, or look at how my clothes are sitting, or feel around my waist, and I’ll keep going over it in my head. Like, why did I eat that, was that too much, do I need to make up for it later.\n\nSo even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, well, if I eat more now then I’m going to be anxious all afternoon or all evening. And sometimes it feels easier to stay hungry than to deal with that fear afterward. I know that may not sound very sensible, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I like to admit. I don’t go around saying to myself, “my worth is my weight,” exactly, because that sounds awful and too simple. But the feeling is there.\n\nWhen I look small, or neat, or kind of contained, I feel like I’m doing something right. Like I’ve kept myself under control, like I’m not taking up too much space, like I’ve managed things properly. It gives me this sense that I’m still acceptable, I suppose.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full — even just after eating, even if logically I know bodies do that — it can really change how I feel about myself. I’ll think, oh no, I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve been careless or weak or something. It’s not just “I don’t like how that looks,” it feels more personal than that, like it says something bad about me.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t think I’d ever judge another person that way. But with myself, yes… staying small makes me feel like I’m handling everything right, and any change in my shape can make me feel like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you try to limit how much you eat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, though it’s a big one. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve already decided what I’m “allowed” before I even sit down.\n\nI cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law — I’ll make sure they have enough, like a real meal, and I fuss over whether they need more of this or that. But then when it’s my turn, by the time I actually sit down, I’ll tell myself, oh, I’m fine with coffee, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or maybe half a piece of toast. I make it sound reasonable in my head, like I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been nibbling while cooking, even when that’s not really true.\n\nIt’s strange because I can see what a normal plate looks like when I’m giving it to someone else. I know how to do that. But for me, it suddenly feels like too much, or unnecessary, or like I should be able to get by on less. So yes, the weight fear is there, but there’s also this habit of cutting it down and convincing myself that a very small amount is enough for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Maybe not in exactly the same way, but I do think I’d still try to keep it controlled.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it out, because the weight and shape part is mixed into everything, but there’s also this feeling that I don’t need what other people need, or that I shouldn’t have a full plate like everyone else. Like at home, I’ll cook for my husband and my mother-in-law, and I can make their plates properly without thinking it’s too much. I’ll put food out for them and make sure they’ve had enough.\n\nBut then when it comes to me, by the time I sit down, I’ll usually tell myself, oh, coffee is fine, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast is enough. And I can make that sound very sensible to myself in the moment, like I’m not really hungry or I don’t need much. So even if I didn’t care about my shape, I think that habit of cutting it down and keeping it small would still be there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I know that probably sounds strange, but yes, I have lost weight, and other people seem to think it’s significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even if other people tell you it's too low?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I suppose I don’t fully know how to answer that, because if I’m just going by what my doctor says, then no, I guess they don’t think my weight is normal. They’ve told me I’ve gotten too thin. And it’s not like there’s no evidence of it, because my work pants really do hang loose now, and I notice that during the day when I’m cleaning or moving around. I’ll have to pull them up or adjust them, and that didn’t used to happen as much.\n\nBut in my own head, it doesn’t feel as simple as, “Oh, I’m too low.” When I see the number on the scale lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what people are telling me, but it’s more like… emotionally, it doesn’t match. Other people say it’s too low, and I can hear that, but then the scale goes down and there’s this little feeling like, okay, that’s better, that’s safer somehow.\n\nSo I guess I can understand why other people say it’s too low. I just have a hard time feeling that it is.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, exactly. The fear is a big part of it, but sometimes it’s also just become this pattern where I tell myself I don’t need as much as everyone else.\n\nLike, I’ll cook a proper plate for my husband and for my mother-in-law, and I’ll make sure theirs looks normal — enough food, something filling, all of that. But then by the time I sit down, I’ll look at what’s left or think about making my own plate, and I’ll just sort of decide coffee is enough for me, or maybe a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been tasting things while cooking, even if that’s not really a meal.\n\nSo yes, gaining weight is there in the background, but it’s not always this loud thought every single time. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve gotten used to giving other people the real meal and then making excuses for why I only need a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. When you say “much larger,” I picture someone eating a whole lot very quickly, like more than a usual meal, and feeling like it was obviously too much. I can’t really think of times like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel uncomfortable or guilty even after eating what someone else would probably say is a normal amount, but that’s not the same as eating a huge amount in a short time. My sense of what’s “a lot” may not be very reliable, honestly, because even small things can feel like a lot to me. But no, I don’t have clear episodes where I’m eating far more than most people would in one sitting or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you feel hungry for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that probably is the main reason, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that plainly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can’t just eat it and move on with my day. I’ll spend the rest of the day thinking about it, like it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll check myself, or look at how my clothes are sitting, or feel around my waist, and I’ll keep going over it in my head. Like, why did I eat that, was that too much, do I need to make up for it later.\n\nSo even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, well, if I eat more now then I’m going to be anxious all afternoon or all evening. And sometimes it feels easier to stay hungry than to deal with that fear afterward. I know that may not sound very sensible, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I like to admit. I don’t go around saying to myself, “my worth is my weight,” exactly, because that sounds awful and too simple. But the feeling is there.\n\nWhen I look small, or neat, or kind of contained, I feel like I’m doing something right. Like I’ve kept myself under control, like I’m not taking up too much space, like I’ve managed things properly. It gives me this sense that I’m still acceptable, I suppose.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full — even just after eating, even if logically I know bodies do that — it can really change how I feel about myself. I’ll think, oh no, I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve been careless or weak or something. It’s not just “I don’t like how that looks,” it feels more personal than that, like it says something bad about me.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t think I’d ever judge another person that way. But with myself, yes… staying small makes me feel like I’m handling everything right, and any change in my shape can make me feel like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you try to limit how much you eat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, though it’s a big one. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve already decided what I’m “allowed” before I even sit down.\n\nI cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law — I’ll make sure they have enough, like a real meal, and I fuss over whether they need more of this or that. But then when it’s my turn, by the time I actually sit down, I’ll tell myself, oh, I’m fine with coffee, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or maybe half a piece of toast. I make it sound reasonable in my head, like I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been nibbling while cooking, even when that’s not really true.\n\nIt’s strange because I can see what a normal plate looks like when I’m giving it to someone else. I know how to do that. But for me, it suddenly feels like too much, or unnecessary, or like I should be able to get by on less. So yes, the weight fear is there, but there’s also this habit of cutting it down and convincing myself that a very small amount is enough for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Maybe not in exactly the same way, but I do think I’d still try to keep it controlled.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it out, because the weight and shape part is mixed into everything, but there’s also this feeling that I don’t need what other people need, or that I shouldn’t have a full plate like everyone else. Like at home, I’ll cook for my husband and my mother-in-law, and I can make their plates properly without thinking it’s too much. I’ll put food out for them and make sure they’ve had enough.\n\nBut then when it comes to me, by the time I sit down, I’ll usually tell myself, oh, coffee is fine, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast is enough. And I can make that sound very sensible to myself in the moment, like I’m not really hungry or I don’t need much. So even if I didn’t care about my shape, I think that habit of cutting it down and keeping it small would still be there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I would. Maybe it would be a little less sharp if I couldn’t literally see it or know the number, but I don’t think the fear would disappear.

Because it isn’t only the mirror or the scale. If I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can feel frightened for the rest of the day that it’s going to show on me somehow. Even if I don’t have proof. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, not always in an obvious way, but I’ll be aware of my stomach, my clothes, how I’m sitting, whether I feel different. I’ll think, “That was too much, I need to make up for it now.”

So even if I couldn’t see myself, I think I’d still imagine it. I’d still be scared that the food had changed me or was about to. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one meal shouldn’t suddenly do that, but in the moment it feels very real.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my regular doctor wanted me to come in because she says I’ve gotten too thin. I guess that’s the main reason. I didn’t really think of it as something for a clinic like this at first, because I still go to work and do what I need to do, but she seemed concerned.\n\nI have noticed things, I suppose. Like my work pants — the ones I wear for cleaning — they’ve been hanging loose on me more than they used to. I keep having to pull them up, and I tell myself they’re just old or stretched out, but… I can see it. And when I get on the scale, if the number is lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds strange, especially when other people are saying I’m too thin, but that’s honestly what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but yes. I do get scared of gaining weight. It’s not even always after some huge meal or anything. If I eat something that feels heavier to me, like biscuits or pasta, then afterward I can’t seem to just move on with the day like a normal person.\n\nI’ll be at work or at home and I keep thinking, “Is it going to show? Can people tell? Is my stomach sticking out?” And I’ll check myself, like how my waistband feels, or I’ll look at myself from the side, or just keep touching at my clothes to see if they feel tighter. It can take over the rest of the day, honestly. Then I start thinking I need to make up for it somehow, because otherwise it feels like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that feels excessive to you in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “excessive” is hard for me to judge, because sometimes even what other people would probably call a normal amount can feel like too much to me.\n\nIf you mean like sitting down and eating a huge amount all at once, I don’t really know that I do that. I can’t think of a clear example where I’d say, yes, that was an unusually large amount in a short time. It’s more that certain foods or portions feel excessive in my head afterward, even if maybe they wouldn’t look that way to someone else. So… I guess I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no, not like that. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating, and I can get really upset in my head about it, but I don’t usually go and make myself vomit.\n\nSometimes I’ve wondered if I’d feel less panicky if I could undo it somehow, but actually doing that isn’t something I can say I do. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably a yes-or-no question, but it’s hard because the feelings after eating can be so strong, even if I’m not vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, do you mean like going for a run or doing a workout specifically because I ate? Because I don’t really have, like, a clear routine where I eat and then say, “Now I have to exercise to burn this off.”\n\nMy work is physical already — I’m on my feet a lot, cleaning and moving around — so sometimes it’s hard for me to separate what’s just part of the day from what would count as exercise. But as far as deliberately exercising right after eating to cancel it out… I don’t know that I’d say I do that, no. It’s more something I worry about in my head than something I can point to as a specific exercise habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are too thin, even when other people tell you that you are?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. That’s the part that gets confusing for me, because my doctor has told me I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it in practical ways, like my work pants don’t fit the same anymore. They hang loose on me, and I know that probably means something.\n\nBut at the same time, when I get on the scale and the number is lower, there’s this part of me that feels relieved. Not exactly happy, or maybe I don’t want to call it happy, but relieved, like I did something right or at least didn’t do something wrong. So when people say I’m too thin, I can hear them, and part of me knows they might be right… but another part of me doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It’s like I can see the looseness in my clothes, but I still don’t trust that I’m “too” thin.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that way to myself, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I spend the rest of the day worried it’s going to show on me somehow. Like I’ll feel it sitting there, and then I start checking myself, my stomach or how my clothes feel, and I keep thinking I need to make up for it. Not always in some clear plan, but in my head it’s just going over and over — like, “Why did I eat that?” or “What if this changes my weight?” It makes me scared, and then restricting feels like the safer thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. I don’t always keep track of it in a very organized way, like writing it down every week or anything, but my doctor has said I’ve gotten too thin, and I can tell something has changed.\n\nMy work pants, for example — they used to fit a certain way, and now they hang loose on me. I notice it when I’m moving around at work, because I’m always bending and walking and cleaning, and I’ll have to adjust them more than I used to. So it’s not like I can say I haven’t noticed.\n\nBut then when I see the number on the scale is lower, part of me feels relieved, which is hard to admit. Even if someone else is saying it’s too much, or that I’m too thin, in my own head it doesn’t land the same way. I know that probably sounds strange, but yes, I have lost weight, and other people seem to think it’s significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even if other people tell you it's too low?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I suppose I don’t fully know how to answer that, because if I’m just going by what my doctor says, then no, I guess they don’t think my weight is normal. They’ve told me I’ve gotten too thin. And it’s not like there’s no evidence of it, because my work pants really do hang loose now, and I notice that during the day when I’m cleaning or moving around. I’ll have to pull them up or adjust them, and that didn’t used to happen as much.\n\nBut in my own head, it doesn’t feel as simple as, “Oh, I’m too low.” When I see the number on the scale lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what people are telling me, but it’s more like… emotionally, it doesn’t match. Other people say it’s too low, and I can hear that, but then the scale goes down and there’s this little feeling like, okay, that’s better, that’s safer somehow.\n\nSo I guess I can understand why other people say it’s too low. I just have a hard time feeling that it is.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, exactly. The fear is a big part of it, but sometimes it’s also just become this pattern where I tell myself I don’t need as much as everyone else.\n\nLike, I’ll cook a proper plate for my husband and for my mother-in-law, and I’ll make sure theirs looks normal — enough food, something filling, all of that. But then by the time I sit down, I’ll look at what’s left or think about making my own plate, and I’ll just sort of decide coffee is enough for me, or maybe a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been tasting things while cooking, even if that’s not really a meal.\n\nSo yes, gaining weight is there in the background, but it’s not always this loud thought every single time. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve gotten used to giving other people the real meal and then making excuses for why I only need a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. When you say “much larger,” I picture someone eating a whole lot very quickly, like more than a usual meal, and feeling like it was obviously too much. I can’t really think of times like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel uncomfortable or guilty even after eating what someone else would probably say is a normal amount, but that’s not the same as eating a huge amount in a short time. My sense of what’s “a lot” may not be very reliable, honestly, because even small things can feel like a lot to me. But no, I don’t have clear episodes where I’m eating far more than most people would in one sitting or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you feel hungry for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that probably is the main reason, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that plainly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can’t just eat it and move on with my day. I’ll spend the rest of the day thinking about it, like it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll check myself, or look at how my clothes are sitting, or feel around my waist, and I’ll keep going over it in my head. Like, why did I eat that, was that too much, do I need to make up for it later.\n\nSo even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, well, if I eat more now then I’m going to be anxious all afternoon or all evening. And sometimes it feels easier to stay hungry than to deal with that fear afterward. I know that may not sound very sensible, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I like to admit. I don’t go around saying to myself, “my worth is my weight,” exactly, because that sounds awful and too simple. But the feeling is there.\n\nWhen I look small, or neat, or kind of contained, I feel like I’m doing something right. Like I’ve kept myself under control, like I’m not taking up too much space, like I’ve managed things properly. It gives me this sense that I’m still acceptable, I suppose.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full — even just after eating, even if logically I know bodies do that — it can really change how I feel about myself. I’ll think, oh no, I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve been careless or weak or something. It’s not just “I don’t like how that looks,” it feels more personal than that, like it says something bad about me.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t think I’d ever judge another person that way. But with myself, yes… staying small makes me feel like I’m handling everything right, and any change in my shape can make me feel like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you try to limit how much you eat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, though it’s a big one. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve already decided what I’m “allowed” before I even sit down.\n\nI cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law — I’ll make sure they have enough, like a real meal, and I fuss over whether they need more of this or that. But then when it’s my turn, by the time I actually sit down, I’ll tell myself, oh, I’m fine with coffee, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or maybe half a piece of toast. I make it sound reasonable in my head, like I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been nibbling while cooking, even when that’s not really true.\n\nIt’s strange because I can see what a normal plate looks like when I’m giving it to someone else. I know how to do that. But for me, it suddenly feels like too much, or unnecessary, or like I should be able to get by on less. So yes, the weight fear is there, but there’s also this habit of cutting it down and convincing myself that a very small amount is enough for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Maybe not in exactly the same way, but I do think I’d still try to keep it controlled.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it out, because the weight and shape part is mixed into everything, but there’s also this feeling that I don’t need what other people need, or that I shouldn’t have a full plate like everyone else. Like at home, I’ll cook for my husband and my mother-in-law, and I can make their plates properly without thinking it’s too much. I’ll put food out for them and make sure they’ve had enough.\n\nBut then when it comes to me, by the time I sit down, I’ll usually tell myself, oh, coffee is fine, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast is enough. And I can make that sound very sensible to myself in the moment, like I’m not really hungry or I don’t need much. So even if I didn’t care about my shape, I think that habit of cutting it down and keeping it small would still be there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be afraid of gaining weight if you couldn't see yourself in a mirror or didn't know your weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I would. Maybe it would be a little less sharp if I couldn’t literally see it or know the number, but I don’t think the fear would disappear.\n\nBecause it isn’t only the mirror or the scale. If I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can feel frightened for the rest of the day that it’s going to show on me somehow. Even if I don’t have proof. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, not always in an obvious way, but I’ll be aware of my stomach, my clothes, how I’m sitting, whether I feel different. I’ll think, “That was too much, I need to make up for it now.”\n\nSo even if I couldn’t see myself, I think I’d still imagine it. I’d still be scared that the food had changed me or was about to. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one meal shouldn’t suddenly do that, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Maybe it would be a little less sharp if I couldn’t literally see it or know the number, but I don’t think the fear would disappear.\n\nBecause it isn’t only the mirror or the scale. If I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can feel frightened for the rest of the day that it’s going to show on me somehow. Even if I don’t have proof. I’ll keep sort of checking myself, not always in an obvious way, but I’ll be aware of my stomach, my clothes, how I’m sitting, whether I feel different. I’ll think, “That was too much, I need to make up for it now.”\n\nSo even if I couldn’t see myself, I think I’d still imagine it. I’d still be scared that the food had changed me or was about to. It sounds silly when I say it out loud, because I know one meal shouldn’t suddenly do that, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think your fear of gaining weight would decrease if you were unable to see your body shape or size?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, but I don’t think it would really go away. I’ve thought about that, because it isn’t always just what I see in the mirror. Sometimes it starts from what I’ve eaten.

Like if I have something that feels “heavier” to me — biscuits, or pasta, things like that — then afterward I can spend the whole rest of the day frightened it’s going to show somehow. Even if I couldn’t see my body properly, I think I’d still be imagining it. I’d be aware of my stomach, how my clothes feel, whether I feel bigger, and I’d keep checking myself in little ways.

And then my mind goes straight to, “I need to make up for that.” So yes, not seeing myself might take away one part of it, but the fear would still be there in my head, because I’d still feel like the food was going to change me.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… my regular doctor wanted me to come in because she says I’ve gotten too thin. I guess that’s the main reason. I didn’t really think of it as something for a clinic like this at first, because I still go to work and do what I need to do, but she seemed concerned.\n\nI have noticed things, I suppose. Like my work pants — the ones I wear for cleaning — they’ve been hanging loose on me more than they used to. I keep having to pull them up, and I tell myself they’re just old or stretched out, but… I can see it. And when I get on the scale, if the number is lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds strange, especially when other people are saying I’m too thin, but that’s honestly what happens in my head.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I don’t like saying it that plainly, but yes. I do get scared of gaining weight. It’s not even always after some huge meal or anything. If I eat something that feels heavier to me, like biscuits or pasta, then afterward I can’t seem to just move on with the day like a normal person.\n\nI’ll be at work or at home and I keep thinking, “Is it going to show? Can people tell? Is my stomach sticking out?” And I’ll check myself, like how my waistband feels, or I’ll look at myself from the side, or just keep touching at my clothes to see if they feel tighter. It can take over the rest of the day, honestly. Then I start thinking I need to make up for it somehow, because otherwise it feels like I’ve done something wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat an amount of food that feels excessive to you in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, “excessive” is hard for me to judge, because sometimes even what other people would probably call a normal amount can feel like too much to me.\n\nIf you mean like sitting down and eating a huge amount all at once, I don’t really know that I do that. I can’t think of a clear example where I’d say, yes, that was an unusually large amount in a short time. It’s more that certain foods or portions feel excessive in my head afterward, even if maybe they wouldn’t look that way to someone else. So… I guess I’m not sure how to answer that exactly.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… no, not like that. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating, and I can get really upset in my head about it, but I don’t usually go and make myself vomit.\n\nSometimes I’ve wondered if I’d feel less panicky if I could undo it somehow, but actually doing that isn’t something I can say I do. I’m sorry, I know that’s probably a yes-or-no question, but it’s hard because the feelings after eating can be so strong, even if I’m not vomiting.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that exactly. I mean, do you mean like going for a run or doing a workout specifically because I ate? Because I don’t really have, like, a clear routine where I eat and then say, “Now I have to exercise to burn this off.”\n\nMy work is physical already — I’m on my feet a lot, cleaning and moving around — so sometimes it’s hard for me to separate what’s just part of the day from what would count as exercise. But as far as deliberately exercising right after eating to cancel it out… I don’t know that I’d say I do that, no. It’s more something I worry about in my head than something I can point to as a specific exercise habit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you are too thin, even when other people tell you that you are?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know. That’s the part that gets confusing for me, because my doctor has told me I’ve gotten too thin, and I can see it in practical ways, like my work pants don’t fit the same anymore. They hang loose on me, and I know that probably means something.\n\nBut at the same time, when I get on the scale and the number is lower, there’s this part of me that feels relieved. Not exactly happy, or maybe I don’t want to call it happy, but relieved, like I did something right or at least didn’t do something wrong. So when people say I’m too thin, I can hear them, and part of me knows they might be right… but another part of me doesn’t feel that way in the moment. It’s like I can see the looseness in my clothes, but I still don’t trust that I’m “too” thin.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict what you eat?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t always say it that way to myself, because it sounds so blunt, but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something heavier, like biscuits or pasta, I spend the rest of the day worried it’s going to show on me somehow. Like I’ll feel it sitting there, and then I start checking myself, my stomach or how my clothes feel, and I keep thinking I need to make up for it. Not always in some clear plan, but in my head it’s just going over and over — like, “Why did I eat that?” or “What if this changes my weight?” It makes me scared, and then restricting feels like the safer thing to do.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, I think so. I don’t always keep track of it in a very organized way, like writing it down every week or anything, but my doctor has said I’ve gotten too thin, and I can tell something has changed.\n\nMy work pants, for example — they used to fit a certain way, and now they hang loose on me. I notice it when I’m moving around at work, because I’m always bending and walking and cleaning, and I’ll have to adjust them more than I used to. So it’s not like I can say I haven’t noticed.\n\nBut then when I see the number on the scale is lower, part of me feels relieved, which is hard to admit. Even if someone else is saying it’s too much, or that I’m too thin, in my own head it doesn’t land the same way. I know that probably sounds strange, but yes, I have lost weight, and other people seem to think it’s significant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal, even if other people tell you it's too low?\n\nPatient: I suppose… I suppose I don’t fully know how to answer that, because if I’m just going by what my doctor says, then no, I guess they don’t think my weight is normal. They’ve told me I’ve gotten too thin. And it’s not like there’s no evidence of it, because my work pants really do hang loose now, and I notice that during the day when I’m cleaning or moving around. I’ll have to pull them up or adjust them, and that didn’t used to happen as much.\n\nBut in my own head, it doesn’t feel as simple as, “Oh, I’m too low.” When I see the number on the scale lower, part of me feels relieved. I know that probably sounds like I’m arguing with what people are telling me, but it’s more like… emotionally, it doesn’t match. Other people say it’s too low, and I can hear that, but then the scale goes down and there’s this little feeling like, okay, that’s better, that’s safer somehow.\n\nSo I guess I can understand why other people say it’s too low. I just have a hard time feeling that it is.\n\nDoctor: Is the fear of gaining weight the only reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, exactly. The fear is a big part of it, but sometimes it’s also just become this pattern where I tell myself I don’t need as much as everyone else.\n\nLike, I’ll cook a proper plate for my husband and for my mother-in-law, and I’ll make sure theirs looks normal — enough food, something filling, all of that. But then by the time I sit down, I’ll look at what’s left or think about making my own plate, and I’ll just sort of decide coffee is enough for me, or maybe a few vegetables, or half a piece of toast. I’ll tell myself I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been tasting things while cooking, even if that’s not really a meal.\n\nSo yes, gaining weight is there in the background, but it’s not always this loud thought every single time. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve gotten used to giving other people the real meal and then making excuses for why I only need a little bit.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a short period of time?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re describing. When you say “much larger,” I picture someone eating a whole lot very quickly, like more than a usual meal, and feeling like it was obviously too much. I can’t really think of times like that.\n\nI mean, sometimes I might feel uncomfortable or guilty even after eating what someone else would probably say is a normal amount, but that’s not the same as eating a huge amount in a short time. My sense of what’s “a lot” may not be very reliable, honestly, because even small things can feel like a lot to me. But no, I don’t have clear episodes where I’m eating far more than most people would in one sitting or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you eat less than you feel hungry for?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that probably is the main reason, if I’m being honest. I don’t always say it to myself that plainly, like “I’m afraid of gaining weight,” but that’s what it comes down to a lot of the time.\n\nIf I eat something that feels heavier to me — like biscuits, or pasta, something like that — I can’t just eat it and move on with my day. I’ll spend the rest of the day thinking about it, like it’s going to show on me somehow. I’ll check myself, or look at how my clothes are sitting, or feel around my waist, and I’ll keep going over it in my head. Like, why did I eat that, was that too much, do I need to make up for it later.\n\nSo even if I’m hungry, I’ll think, well, if I eat more now then I’m going to be anxious all afternoon or all evening. And sometimes it feels easier to stay hungry than to deal with that fear afterward. I know that may not sound very sensible, but in the moment it feels very real.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is the main thing that determines your self-worth?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, more than I like to admit. I don’t go around saying to myself, “my worth is my weight,” exactly, because that sounds awful and too simple. But the feeling is there.\n\nWhen I look small, or neat, or kind of contained, I feel like I’m doing something right. Like I’ve kept myself under control, like I’m not taking up too much space, like I’ve managed things properly. It gives me this sense that I’m still acceptable, I suppose.\n\nBut if I look in the mirror and my stomach looks even a little full — even just after eating, even if logically I know bodies do that — it can really change how I feel about myself. I’ll think, oh no, I’ve let myself go. Like I’ve been careless or weak or something. It’s not just “I don’t like how that looks,” it feels more personal than that, like it says something bad about me.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I don’t think I’d ever judge another person that way. But with myself, yes… staying small makes me feel like I’m handling everything right, and any change in my shape can make me feel like I’ve failed somehow.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the only reason you try to limit how much you eat?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s the only reason, though it’s a big one. Sometimes it’s more like I’ve already decided what I’m “allowed” before I even sit down.\n\nI cook proper plates for my husband and my mother-in-law — I’ll make sure they have enough, like a real meal, and I fuss over whether they need more of this or that. But then when it’s my turn, by the time I actually sit down, I’ll tell myself, oh, I’m fine with coffee, or I’ll just have a few vegetables, or maybe half a piece of toast. I make it sound reasonable in my head, like I’m not that hungry, or I’ve been nibbling while cooking, even when that’s not really true.\n\nIt’s strange because I can see what a normal plate looks like when I’m giving it to someone else. I know how to do that. But for me, it suddenly feels like too much, or unnecessary, or like I should be able to get by on less. So yes, the weight fear is there, but there’s also this habit of cutting it down and convincing myself that a very small amount is enough for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still try to control your food intake if you didn't care about your weight or body shape?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, probably. Maybe not in exactly the same way, but I do think I’d still try to keep it controlled.\n\nIt’s hard to separate it out, because the weight and shape part is mixed into everything, but there’s also this feeling that I don’t need what other people need, or that I shouldn’t have a full plate like everyone else.
